1. See Reuter, op. cit., note 5, Chapter 16.
2. See note 5, Chapter 15.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 348 ---
‘Reports of our Death are Greatly
Exaggerated’
The New York families are on their knees. The Mafia is falling
apart. It’s collapsing under our blows.
FBI agent, 1988
Since Crime Inc. was first broadcast almost 25 years ago, most
people with even just a casual interest in American affairs will have
got the impression that traditional organized crime – La Cosa
Nostra, a.k.a. the American Mafia – has been ‘wiped out’. They will
have absorbed a few tales of heroic mob-busters jailing the most
notorious bosses, and will probably assume this means that the old
crime families are finished, done for, ‘out for the count’. Even
dedicated Mafia buffs are likely to believe that Italian-Americans no
longer dominate Gangsterdom USA, because of the coincidental
impact of five debilitating factors: the massive crack-down by law
enforcement; numerous defections to the Witness Protection
Program; a far higher murder rate within the families; Anno Domini,
the fact that so many ancient godfathers have finally died; and the
ever-increasing competition of criminals springing from more recent
immigrant groups – Hispanic, Oriental, Russian.
There is some truth in this impression, but not a lot. Just as Mark
Twain once had to cable a news agency, ‘The report of my death
was an exaggeration’, so current ‘Godfathers’, in or out of jail, can
claim correctly that their brotherhood is still very much in business.
The notion of a Mob verging on extinction has taken hold largely
because of a decade of highly publicized trials based on RICO – the
Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations statute.1 The most
prestigious was the 1986 ‘Commission Trial’, in which the bosses of
four of New York’s five families were accused of participating in a
‘Commission’ governing the affairs of the entire Cosa Nostra
structure across America – in effect the mob’s ‘board of directors’
coast-to-coast. The idea of nailing the Commission as a continuing

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 349 ---
criminal enterprise fitting the RICO definition seems so obvious as
to be unremarkable, yet it took no less a prosecutor than Rudolph
Giuliani, then US Attorney for the Southern District of New York
(later the City’s Republican Mayor) to grasp the concept. Giuliani
had read Joe Bonanno’s autobiography, A Man Of Honor, in which
the former boss of the family bearing his name recalled how the
Commission was founded back in 1931. He also laid out its
structure and functions in the clearest terms. The book provoked
Giuliano to remark, ‘If Bonanno can write about a Commission, I
can indict it’.
Under RICO it is not enough to show the existence of a criminal
enterprise: it is necessary to prove at least two specific acts of
racketeering which amount to a pattern. In the Commission Trial
ten senior Commission figures were charged with running an
extortion and price-fixing racket controlling New York’s concrete
industry. They faced thirteen counts of extorting money from
contractors and six of bribery. Two defendants were charged with
furthering Commission aims through a loansharking conspiracy.
Three Commission bosses were variously charged over a total of
five murders. The most spectacular slaying was that of Bonanno
family boss Carmine Galante in 1979, which the other bosses had
condoned because he stood in the way of their scheme to reorganize
that strife-ridden clan. The bosses were linked to the murder
through Tony Indelicato, a Bonanno capo. He was not identified at
the scene of the shooting – the garden of a restaurant in
Knickerbocker Avenue, Brooklyn – but his palm-print was found in
the killers’ getaway car. Later that night he was caught on video-
tape outside the Revenite Social Club in Little Italy, Manhattan,
receiving congratulations from other Mafiosi.
The leading ‘Commission’ defendants were Tony Salerno, Tony
Corallo, Carmine Persico and Phil Rastelli, bosses of the Genovese,
Lucchese, Colombo and Bonanno families respectively. When they
were first charged, back in February 1985, Paul Castellano and
Aniello Dellacroce, boss and underboss of the Gambino family, had
also been indicted, but both died in December that year –
Dellacroce of cancer and Castellano, two weeks later, of murder.
Had they lived, the godfathers of all five New York families would
have been on trial together.
When the trial began in September 1986, evidence was produced
from a dazzling array of sources. Bugs had been planted in the

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 350 ---
bosses’ homes, offices and social clubs. In a bold coup by New
York’s State Organized Crime Task Force, one device had even been
secreted in the Jaguar of Tony ‘Ducks’ Corallo. This later caught the
Lucchese boss, overcome by a spasm of old-fashioned Mafia
morality, planning to murder other LCN members involved in drug-
dealing. Such gems helped prove the long-suspected truth that
bosses on the Commission could snuff out lives almost at whim.
Bolder still, one FBI agent had gone deep undercover for six
years and become a close associate of the Bonanno clan. When the
agent, Joseph Pistone, at last pulled out, he was close to initiation
as a family member! His efforts yielded a mass of intelligence on
the inner workings of a Mafia family while it was being torn apart
through civil war. At the trial both Pistone (known to the mob as
Donnie Brasco) and Joey Cantalupo (star of our series, Crime Inc.)
testified about Cosa Nostra rituals, traditions and rivalries, but the
most authoritative witness was Angelo Lonardo, former underboss
of the Cleveland family, by now almost extinct. He recalled his own
initiation in a suite in Cleveland’s Statler Hotel in the 1940s, and he
confirmed the existence of the Commission.
They make all the rules and regulations, what you can do, what
you can’t do … For instance, nobody can sell narcotics. That
would bring too much heat on anybody. You always have to do
the right thing. You can’t have no prostitutes. The rules are that
they [members] can’t kill a boss in other cities or in New York
City without them [the Commission] knowing anything about it.
Before they do anything they got to get permission.2
Lonardo explained how Cleveland communicated with the
Commission through the Genovese family, whose current leader
Tony Salerno was watching him give evidence. Lonardo also
revealed his own murderous role in a fifty-year saga of Mafia
killings in Cleveland, many committed without Commission
authority.
In Tony Corallo’s defence, his lawyer admitted that both La Cosa
Nostra and the Commission do exist but, he maintained, not to
conduct crime. As for the concrete racket, some defendants
conceded there was a lucrative cartel and that pay-offs had been
made to a bag-man, Ralph Scopo, who was also on trial. Yet they
argued that the contractors had given the pay-offs freely, in return

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 351 ---
for huge benefits. If anyone should be on trial it was not the LCN
bosses but these businessmen: for bid-rigging which was, they
claimed, the only conspiracy revealed by the evidence. According to
the defence, there was no evidence linking the Commission with
any specific crime.
An unimpressed jury convicted the defendants on all racketeering
charges. The judge jailed each one for a hundred years, except for
Tony Indelicato who got off with a mere forty. The Grim Reaper
will ensure that none serve their full sentences. ‘Fat Tony’ Salerno
has already died in prison.
In another ground-breaking case, known as the ‘Pizza
Connection’ and brought by the same New York Southern District,
one of the twenty-two defendants died even before the jury was
sent out. He had been murdered during the seventeen months of
America’s longest ever mob trial. Why ‘Pizza Connection’? Because
several defendants were using pizzerias across America as fronts for
distributing vast amounts of heroin imported from Sicily. The case
proved in court what informed observers had known for years: that
the most run-down LCN families of eastern America were being
transfused by more energetic and aggressive elements from the
Mafia’s motherland.
The lead defendant was Gaetano Badlamenti, who had once been
head of Sicily’s Mafia Commission, or ‘Cupola’. He had fled from
Sicily back in the 1970s to escape its Mafia wars, in which a rising
clan from the town of Corleone was murdering many of his blood
relatives. Fleeing to Brazil, he managed to re-establish himself by
exporting cocaine to the USA through relatives in Michigan and
Wisconsin. They performed as investors and distributors while
others acted as couriers. Seven of these relatives were now standing
trial with him.
Twelve more defendants belonged to another gang of Sicilian
Mafiosi, headed by a capo in the Bonanno family named Salvatore
Catalano.
He
was
charged
with
importing
cocaine
from
Badlamenti’s team in Brazil and heroin from the Corleonesi in
Sicily. How Catalano managed to work with both these deadliest of
enemies at the same time is still a mystery. The case had originated
six years earlier, in 1979. Like the ‘Commission’ case, it was tied in
with the murder of Bonanno family boss, Carmine Galante. Except
for Tony Indelicato, the team which killed Galante were all ‘Zips’,
the term used by American Mafiosi to describe new Sicilian

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 352 ---
immigrants. They were keen to ‘whack’ Galante because he was
laying claim to the entire New York-Sicily heroin racket. When FBI
agents in New York looked into this killing they found out their
inquiries were overlapping with FBI and DEA probes in other cities.
It also coincided with investigations already being pursued by anti-
Mafia squads in Italy. In an unprecedented show of trust, the Italian
squads and the FBI joined forces.
By autumn 1983, the investigators had been authorized to
conduct over seventy separate wiretaps, even on public pay-phones
in America and Italy. This breakthrough was crucial because now
most wiseguys on both sides of the Atlantic knew that any phones
in their homes, offices and clubs were almost certainly bugged.
Scattered in among 100,000 monitored calls was the proof of plans
to transfer massive amounts of drugs, disguised in the conversation
as consignments of ‘shirts’, ‘suits’, ‘trousers’, ‘shoes’, ‘potatoes’,
‘cheese’ or just plain ‘parcels’. At times ‘boys’ meant heroin and
‘girls’ meant cocaine. To the eavesdroppers all this was readily
decodable, but it was too complicated for some conspirators, who
became desperately confused over exactly what goods they were
talking about.
The investigation had been boosted in 1983 when a Sicilian
Mafioso named Tommaso Buscetta was captured in Brazil. Later he
would wax lyrical about the Honoured Society in which, he
claimed, he had been a leading and honourable figure before even
more ruthless hoodlums took it over and killed two of Buscetta’s
blameless sons.3 It was these same Corleonesi who had murdered
Gaetano Badlamenti’s equally innocent relatives as well as hundreds
of rival Mafiosi. Sickened by this slaughter, and to save his own
skin, Buscetta now became a most effective informer against the
Mafia on both sides of the Atlantic.
It was Buscetta who had fingered Badlamenti by revealing he
was operating his cocaine racket from Brazil and liaising with Sal
Catalano in New York. Phone intercepts eventually yielded enough
information to secure Badlamenti’s arrest in Spain in April 1984,
triggering simultaneous raids next day on dozens of Pizza
Connection conspirators. The resulting trial, United States v
Badlamenti, would last from October 1985 until February 1987.
As prosecutor Robert Stewart told the jury, the case had two
themes: not just ‘the smuggling of massive quantities of heroin and
cocaine into the US on a protracted and systematic basis’ but also

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 353 ---
‘the smuggling of massive amounts of US currency out of the US to
the overseas suppliers’. In little over a year just one of these
operations would have imported a ton and a half of pure heroin
into New York, worth one third of a billion dollars wholesale. After
six days’ deliberation, the long-suffering jurors convicted all but one
of the defendants. The judge sentenced the ringleaders to between
twenty and thirty-five years in jail.
The Pizza Connection trial laid bare both the Bonanno family’s
weakness and its rejuvenation through an aggressive and relatively
young Sicilian faction. It showed how the injunction against drug-
trafficking (always as honoured in the breach as in the observance)
was
now
being
flagrantly
flouted.
It
also
showed
how
internationalized ‘OC’ had become, for the conspiracy embraced not
just America, Italy and Brazil but many other countries. Yet, from
the eulogistic press coverage, and even the title of a sound and
conscientious book on the investigation – The Last Days of the
Sicilians4 – it seemed the most significant aspect was that US law
enforcement, with help from Italy, had finally crushed the mob.
That impression was reinforced by many more RICO prosecutions
against Mafia families across America, climaxing back in New York
with the trial of the flamboyantly notorious John Gotti in 1992.
Gotti had emerged as boss of the Gambino family after the previous
boss, Paul Castellano, was gunned down in 1985. It now seems
clear that Gotti had ordered the hit because ‘Big Paulie’ had
discovered (from surveillance tape transcripts made public in
another case) that Gotti and his crew were trafficking in drugs on
the side. According to Castellano’s standing orders, any family
members caught drug-trafficking had to be killed. He did not apply
this to himself, as he was happy to take drug money from the
Sicilians who had killed Galante with his permission. As US
prosecutor (now FBI Director) Louis Freeh put it, ‘Paul Castellano
got paid, in spite of threatening others with death for dealing’.
Instead of just killing Gotti, Castellano made the mistake of
telling him in advance that he would be breaking up his crew. As if
to appeal this decision, Gotti set up a meeting with him at Sparks
Steakhouse in Manhattan. When Castellano arrived, he and his
chauffeur were shot dead.
After killing his way to the top, in the late 1980s Gotti
humiliated law enforcement with spectacular acquittals in three
successive trials. Now he was known as the ‘Dapper Don’ because of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 354 ---
his immaculate style, and the ‘Teflon Don’ because no charges
would ever stick on him. Eventually he was confronted with a RICO
rap alleging illegal gambling, loansharking, obstruction of justice,
and the ordering of six people’s murders, including those of
Castellano and Robert DiBernardo, the Gambino member who was
also America’s most successful trader in pornography.5
What turned the odds against Gotti walking free yet again was
the switch of his consigliere, Sammy ‘the Bull’ Gravano, from
codefendant to government witness. Originally Gravano had been
charged with three murders. Now he was admitting sixteen more, as
well as a range of racketeering crimes. The ethics of doing a deal
with such a man are highly questionable (as was the mere five-year
term he would receive for all nineteen murders!) but any qualms
were outweighed by the prosecution’s zeal to nail Gotti once and
for all. Gravano testified for nine days about everything from his
initiation to the family’s daily operations, its 400-strong structure
and the plot to kill Castellano. Gotti was also hooked on what he
had said on hundreds of hours of highly incriminating tapes of his
conversations in his apartment above the Ravenite Social Club in
Mulberry Street. On one occasion he was overheard lamenting
about recordings which had been disclosed in an earlier court case:
‘I tell you what a fuckin’ heartbreak. You know you feel like you’re
being raped with these fuckin’ tapes.’6
With all this evidence against him, it was no surprise that the
jury found Gotti guilty and the judge sentenced him and his
underboss, Frank Locascio, to life imprisonment.
It had become a stark necessity for the Justice Department to jail
Gotti because his air of invincibility was undermining the message –
painstakingly worked on for over a decade and at great expense –
that LCN was no longer a major force in American life. Now, with
the Dapper Don locked up in the nation’s most secure prison – and
even though he was said still to be running the Gambino family
through his son, John Junior – the message was at last believable:
the FBI had broken the Mob. No more the Teflon Don, he was
renamed the ‘Velcro Don’ because at last the charges had stuck.
A few decades ago some leading London detectives followed this
rule: ‘Before destroying the enemy, you must first invent it’. To
boost their own reputations these Scotland Yard officers used their
chums in the press to inflate the ferocity of their target hoodlums,
so that when they locked the villains away, their achievement

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 355 ---
would seem all the greater. Today it is clear that these gangs existed
more in print than they ever did in reality. Likewise cynical New
Yorkers might now be tempted to observe that if Gotti had not
existed it would have been necessary to invent him. So awful a
human being, so arrogantly dismissive of the efforts of decent
citizens to bring him down, Gotti had come to resemble Superman’s
relentlessly evil adversary, Lex Luther.
Gotti did not just order the whacking of many Mafia cronies. He
would even sanction killing straight citizens. On 18 March 1980,
John Favara was driving home along the street where he and Gotti
both lived when a twelve-year-old boy on a bike shot out from
behind a garbage dumpster. Dazzled by the setting sun, Favara
drove into the boy, who died on impact. To Favara’s double
distress, the boy turned out to be Frank Gotti, John’s younger son.
Even though this was plainly an accident, it was also plain that if
Favara did not vanish voluntarily, he would soon be vanished by
avenging allies of the distraught don. Sure enough, on the day
Favara was finally due to move out after all of two months, he was
abducted and never seen again. Underworld information has it that
Gotti himself cut up Favara – dead or alive – with a chainsaw. Years
later, this image of a grieving Godfather ordering the death of his
son’s killer acquired awesome nobility – outrageous but so
inevitably the action of a Mafia godfather that many New Yorkers
could accept it as almost just. Once people like Gotti acquire role
model status – and he was now enjoying America’s biggest gangster
personality cult since Al Capone – they can become a far bigger
threat to law and order than their crimes alone would justify. Their
imprisonment is no longer a moral obligation. It becomes a political
necessity. Gotti just had to go – and ‘go’ he did, as spectacularly as
the villain in an old-fashioned melodrama. Now he’s gone, the
entire US Mafia looks finished. But this is an illusion, a trick
perpetrated on the American public just as magically deceptive as
anything performed by the illusionist David Copperfield.
It is beyond dispute that in the past dozen years most of
America’s Mafia families have taken a pounding. The Cleveland
family barely exists, while almost every Philadelphia mobster who
hasn’t been murdered is in jail. Philadelphia is where Omertà – the
code of silence – has fallen into total disrepute: no less than six
family members have become federal witnesses. This shambles has
been followed by ‘a violent re-awakening’ in the clan and the

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 356 ---
emergence of a native Sicilian, John Stanfa, as boss. This is a
luckless job: in 1995 Stanfa was convicted on racketeering charges.
In many other cities – Boston, Buffalo, Kansas City, Los Angeles,
Miami, Newark and New Orleans – there have been successful
prosecutions against the Mob. In addition, vast industrial rackets,
dominated by LCN figures for decades, seem to have been wrested
from their control. At last the International Brotherhood of
Teamsters has a president, Ron Carey, who is not a Mafia nominee.
This is due partly to the thirty-year struggle of rank-and-file groups
to return the union to its members, and partly to strategic
prosecutions aimed at ensuring that the Teamsters’ massive pension
funds are no longer milch-cows for organized crime. At one point
the entire union was charged with being a ‘criminal enterprise’
fitting the RICO definition.
Similar efforts have proved successful at local level. Since 1986
Tony Provenzano’s old mob-infested Local 560 has been placed
under federal trusteeship. However, the Provenzano clan’s grip
remains so strong that it took until 1992 before the brave and
tenacious court-appointed trustee, Ed Stier, manoeuvred the
leadership clear of what a judge clearly identified as the influence
of the Genovese crime family.7
The Mob has lost another traditional source of revenue, the
casino industry of Las Vegas, as huge and generally respectable
corporations have moved in to replace the old private gambling
operators. In Atlantic City, New Jersey state law has helped prevent
the families from owning casinos. For some years the families took
a slice of the action through the unions which supplied construction
workers and hotel staff, but here too Mafia influence was curbed
when, in 1991, a court decision put a federal monitor in charge of
Local 54 of the hotel and restaurant workers’ union (HEREIU). Now
a federal monitor has been imposed on the same union nationwide,
in an attempt to end the mob’s continuing control through Chicago
and New York.
Traditional ‘OC’ might also appear threatened by the expansion
of even more ruthless alien gangster organizations, now well
established in the USA. Even if there had been no Pizza Connection
probe, the Mafia would have lost overall control of America’s
heroin market because Mexican, Chinese and Vietnamese gangs all
now have direct methods of smuggling the drug from source to
wholesaler. As for cocaine, Hispanics – above all the ferocious

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 357 ---
Colombian cartels – now dominate a market worth $40 billion a
year, of which the Mafia seems to be taking almost nothing.
So is La Cosa Nostra really done for? That is the view of shrewd
observers such as Peter Reuter.8 Drawing a parallel with the
collapse of the Soviet Union (created only three years before
organized crime’s greatest break, Prohibition), Dr Reuter says ‘The
Mafia is almost extinguished now as a major actor in the United
States’ criminal world.’ Citing many signs of LCN decay – almost
whacked out of narcotics, deprived of its old rackets in several legal
markets, with scores of members in jail and scores more murdered
or turned federal witness – he also points out that New York’s five
families now have only 1,200 finger-pricked initiates, compared
with 3,000 in the 1970s.
But does a fall in made men necessarily mean the brotherhood is
in terminal decline? Has it lost all its major sources of income? Does
taking a beating over drugs and being shut out from some lucrative
‘legit’ businesses mean the Mob really isn’t what it used to be?
Almost from its beginnings back in 1931 the Commission has
banned drug-trafficking. Even though the rule has continually been
ignored, at least the godfathers saw fit to create it and for a very
good reason: not moral revulsion but strategic wisdom. Drugs
always bring ‘heat’. Big city police forces rarely concern themselves
with illegal gambling or loan-sharking because these rackets have
few visible consequences. The consequences of drugs are only too
visible: on streets, in violent crime statistics and in wrecked bodies,
even in the homes of wealthy opinion-makers distraught at the
spaced-out brains of their expensively educated brats. Sooner or
later, politicians national and local will demand action against
major traffickers. Just as in the 1940s and 1950s, Bureau of
Narcotics Director Harry Anslinger saw that the only way to
overcome FBI inertia over organized crime was to trumpet the
Mafia’s roll in drugs, so in the 1980s it was public concern over
drugs that allowed so many federal agents and so much money to
be allocated to the Pizza Connection.
So let us consider the possibility that today’s Mafia bosses – in
America if not Sicily – have re-learned the lesson taught by their
forefathers in the 1930s: stay away from drugs, and then cops, even
straight ones, will usually stay away from you. What’s the point of
chasing after vast profits from drugs if you risk losing your liberty
for at least twenty years? The Mafia’s lack of involvement in

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 358 ---
cocaine and its shrinking role in heroin may prove not that it is in
irretrievable decline but that it has arrived at a strategy for survival.
This is just the point made by two eminent OC-watchers, Charles
Rogovin and Frederick Martens, who draw a parallel with the world
of Wall Street: ‘The evidence suggests LCN has chosen to limit its
exposure in the very risky derivatives or junk bond market –
narcotics – and has stuck with mutual funds and blue chip stocks –
gambling and loansharking’, where, they say, the Mafia is as strong
as ever.9
And what of falling membership? Again, the Mafia’s traditional
strength lay partly in its exclusivity. For many years the books were
closed. When they were re-opened the initiates included many
incompetents, misfits and – it later turned out – traitors only too
willing to testify against their former goombahs. On the ‘Gotti tape’
for 4 January 1990, Sammy Gravano (before his defection) is heard
lamenting that he does not know the full names of the men which
he, Gotti and Frank Locascio are about to initiate into the Gambino
family. ‘I don’t have their last names. I don’t have the proper
spelling.’ John Gotti is unfazed and sloppily suggests that maybe
‘Fat Dom’ will be enough! Not that Gotti wants to initiate ‘Fat Dom’,
whom he brands ‘triple scum’. He also talks of making an old guy
called ‘Poncho’: ‘We did have that “Poncho” on the fuckin’ list. I
keep forgetting his fuckin’ name.’10 So much for careful research
into the backgrounds, character and earning power of the
candidates.
The number of made men in a family is no sure indicator of its
wealth, current turnover or inner strength. What counts is the
quality of the recruits. In this the families resemble many of
America’s biggest legitimate corporations which just a few years
ago posted massive losses and discovered there was no safety in a
vast payroll. They have responded by ‘down-sizing’ – sacking
thousands of staff and shedding loss-making subsidiaries – and have
emerged trimmer, fitter and profitable again. Small is beautiful.
What’s good for America is, as ever, good for the Mob!
The notion of a Mob in decline partly reflects media
bedazzlement with law enforcement’s attack on just two families:
the Bonannos and the Gambinos, above all John Gotti. Yet at the
same time the Genovese family – traditionally the biggest and most
senior clan of all – has survived largely unscathed. Sure, its boss
Tony Salerno was sent to jail and died there, but he was already

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 359 ---
past retirement age. Otherwise this outfit (more properly called the
Lucky Luciano family) is still going strong. While the Gambino
family has shrunk from 400 to 200 active members in five years,
the Genovese has remained constant at 300.11 It has also moved in
on much of the Gambinos’ construction and gambling territory,
worth many millions of dollars a year. The vast spread of these
enterprises was clearly laid out by John Gotti when he was
complaining about Sammy Gravano’s manic empire-building.12
Sammy, slow it down. Pull it in a fuckin’ notch! You come up
with fifteen companies, for Christsake! You got rebar; you got
concrete pouring; you got Italian floors now. You got
construction; you got drywall; you got asbestos; you got rugs.
What the fuck next?
With Gravano turned stool-pigeon and Gotti locked up for life, these
kinds of enterprises have been ‘inherited’ by the Genovese, which
still controls all its old rackets, like New York’s Fulton Fish Market,
as it has done for eighty years, most notoriously during World War
Two when Jo ‘Socks’ Lanza helped deliver the waterfront labour
force to the Allied cause to secure Luciano’s eventual release.13
When part of the Market went up in flames in March 1995, there
was immediate speculation that this was arson-cum-extortion
committed on behalf of the Genovese. In October 1995 New York’s
Mayor Rudi Giuliani – the same Giuliani who had slaughtered the
mob as a US Attorney – moved to replace members of the mob-
controlled market union with clean labour. The immediate results
were a collapse in fish distribution throughout New York and a lot
of disgruntled restaurateurs.
The Genovese family isn’t confined to its old redoubts. Even a
new Manhattan landmark, the Jacob Javits Convention Centre,
which opened in 1986, largely ‘belongs’ to the Genovese clan. As
recently as March 1995, all the carpenters seemed to be Genovese
nominees. Back in 1991 police found a list of many of those same
carpenters during a covert visit to the HQ of Vincent ‘the Chin’
Gigante, boss of the Genovese family since Tony Salerno’s death.
The Centre is also thickly populated by folk from New York’s
four other families. In the words of Teamster President Ron Carey,
the Centre is ‘a United Nations for the Mob’. By imposing
extortionate labour costs, they have made the Centre America’s

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 360 ---
most expensive exhibition venue by far. So, despite law
enforcement’s efforts, it is still possible for Mafiosi not just to
maintain their stranglehold on existing rackets – they are as strong
as ever in garbage collection – but to move into new ones. And for
all the sound and fury of a decade of anti-Mafia trials, no senior
Genovese member has ever become a federal witness.
Nor has every anti-Mafia trial gone the government’s way. Major
initiatives have failed. In 1988 prosecutors aimed to jail twenty
members and associates of the Lucchese family in New Jersey: the
entire hierarchy of a hugely profitable wing of one of America’s
most tightly knit Mafia families were to be put out of circulation.
After nearly two years in court, however, all the defendants were
cleared. Several were later convicted of lesser crimes, but this
verdict showed that juries would no longer tolerate vast
investigations  in which FBI agents ‘get into bed’ with low-lifes no
better than the mobsters they are testifying against.14
In New York in 1994 and 1995 two entire slates of Colombo
mobsters (fourteen men in all) were acquitted largely because the
jury took a dim view of the fact that for years an FBI agent had
been feeding critical intelligence to his informant inside the
Colombo family. This was a capo named Greg Scarpa who had used
FBI intelligence to wage his own internal war and provoke crimes.
One juror observed that the defendants were just ‘a group of guys
trying to stay alive’, whereas the higher-ranking ‘Scarpa was
running free doing what he wanted to do’, including murder.
On closer inspection, therefore, many sure signs that the Mafia is
finished are not so sure after all. But even if the traditional family is
finished, that does not mean the Mafia way of doing business is
done for, or that there will never be as many Mafiosi. There was a
Mafia – indeed there were many ‘mafias’– in American long before
there were families in the form layed down in 1931. The only
reason the Mafia adopted the Commission system was because it
suited many competing gangs to create an arbitration system to
deal with territorial disputes. Neither the Commission nor the
families were meant to be regarded as more important than the two
basic aims of all concerned: to make lots of money and stay out of
jail.
Since RICO prosecutions of LCN families have become by far the
most effective method of locking Italian-American gangsters away,
a younger generation ought already to have realized that being

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 361 ---
‘made’ isn’t worth risking half a lifetime in jail. There was a time
when LCN’s links with corrupt cops and judges were so strong that
family membership was the best insurance against going to jail.
Now that no such advantage holds, there would seem little point in
joining.
British readers will be aware of all those people who were
deluded into becoming ‘Names’ at the international insurance
market known as Lloyds of London, only to lose millions. These
suckers crazily assumed that promises of high returns were worth
risking limitless liability. Today it seems that only ‘mugs’ – or
people who have more money than sense – would become Names at
Lloyds. Likewise, any Mafioso with brains would run a mile from an
invitation to prick his finger within a circle of friends. The risk isn’t
worth the candle-wax.
This does not mean the Mafia is doomed. It just means that in
future many Mafiosi will work freelance. They may be aligned,
remotely, with a crime family but they won’t be ‘on the books’. A
looser, floating, extended Mafia will only be a reversion to the
tradition which prevailed for centuries before anyone dreamed up a
commission. Italian scholars have long rejected the idea of the
Mafia as a formal secret society. Social research ‘has probably now
reached the point where we can say that the Mafia, as the term is
commonly understood, does not exist’. So writes Pino Arlacchi who
points out that, as late as the 1940s, in western Sicily and southern
Calabria ‘the Mafia was a form of behaviour and a kind of power,
not a formal organization … To behave as a mafioso was to behave
honourably. It was to conform … to certain rules of cunning,
courage and ferocity, of robbery and fraud’.15 In support, Arlacchi
cites a nineteenth-century writer, Pitré, who stated, ‘The Mafia is
neither a sect nor an association, and has neither rules nor statutes
… The Mafia is a certain consciousness of one’s own being, an
exaggerated notion of individual force and strength.’
If we are to believe the pentito, Tommaso Buscetta, it was only in
1957 that mafiosi in Sicily first formed a commission or ‘cupola’. It
always was the case, even in America, that shrewd individuals on
the fringes of the Mafia could make more money outside a family
than they might within it. This held true not just for nonor half-
Italians who could never be ‘made’ but even for full Italians who
were eligible but deftly avoided any invitation. Such individuals
have usually handed part of their profits to the Mafia in tribute or

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 362 ---
in return for protection. However, just as merely being ‘made’ has
never guaranteed that a mobster will make money, so not being a
member has never in itself stopped anyone capable of accumulating
substantial criminal wealth from doing so.
Look at it another way. The Mafia had survived for centuries as a
kind of guerilla movement, a loose confederation of rural brigands
and urban racketeers. For sure they were criminals but, in the eyes
of their Sicilian compari, they legitimised themselves by opposing
foreign oppressors and absentee landlords. Faced with standing
armies, the guerilla approach was the only one open to them, but in
1930s America there was no army against them, only sporadic
opposition from local cops, while J. Edgar Hoover opted his FBI out.
Similarly in Italy in the 1950s the Mafiosi faced no concerted
onslaught from police or prosecutors or from politicians who were
more likely to be working with them! In both countries their leaders
were emboldened to form their own armies – complete with
military ranks. But in the mid-1980s America committed brigades of
federal officers to all-out war on the Mafia and armed them with
the ‘nuke’ power of wiretaps and RICO. At this level of
confrontation the Mafia’s ‘armies’ were bound to get crushed.
For the foreseeable future shrewd Mafiosi are likely to revert to
the brigand role and drop out of quasi-military structures. They will
still be bonded together, not by rituals or oaths but by shared
lineage, common criminal interests and a common enemy.
Certainly, informal cells of Mafiosi could still be charged under
RICO (just as hundreds of ‘legitimate’ businessmen with no LCN
connections have suffered RICO convictions in the past decade). But
it might not be so easy to brand them as belonging to a continuing
criminal enterprise, or for the FBI to stick them up on family charts.
What’s more, they could no longer be killed for revealing the secrets
of a society which they have never joined!
Perhaps the resilient multi-ethnic syndicate of Chicago points the
way. Although LCN analysts have been drawing up Chicago crime
family charts for thirty years, these never looked quite right,
because they had to include Greeks, blacks and even the Welshman
Murray Humphreys. Far from weakening the outfit, this racial
diversity seems only to have strengthened it. The Italians don’t
seem to have seriously questioned the high-level roles of non-
Italians, but this reflects the irregularity of an operation which, as
far back as Al Capone and its other Neapolitan bosses, has never

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 363 ---
insisted on ritual initiations in the Sicilian style. This was confirmed
by the jailed Chicago capo, Joey Lombardo, in a 1990 deposition: ‘I
never pricked my fingers and never took an oath over guns or over
swords and never put burning paper in my hand … I never took an
oath or any ceremony of any secret organization’.16
One place where Italian-American gangsters have already stood
down the Mafia is north-east Pennsylvania, the traditional
stronghold of the Russell Bufalino family. Today this family is
almost extinct, but that does not mean Mafia-style ‘OC’ is defunct in
that area. Far from it. In Scranton a blood family called Baldassari
has operated for over forty years with all the defiance and a
swagger of initiated Mafiosi without ever belonging to a formal
crime LCN family.17 Involved in gambling, loansharking and
extortion, its leader Elmo Baldassari has never been in awe of made
men. Indeed he thought nothing of ejecting Russell Bufalino
himself, along with a supporting cast of wiseguys, from one of his
bars for making too much noise late at night. Sure, Elmo could not
call on Mafia help when he was busted in 1991 for extortion – he
had to plead guilty and was jailed for two years – but he would
have received far heavier punishment if he had belonged to an LCN
family hit by a RICO prosecution. As it is, the Baldassaris seem
likely to outlast the Mafia in this region. Not that honest
Scrantonians will notice any difference.
For the time being, it seems, even Mafia families which are in
good shape have stopped making new members. This is partly to
restore Omertà and reduce the risk of future Gravanos, Buscettas
and Fratiannos; partly to develop higher quality candidates; and
partly to allow law enforcement’s zeal to subside. This is not so
dumb a strategy. Washington’s enthusiasm for dedicated LCN-
busting has been waning since before 1990 when the entire coast-
to-coast network of fifteen Strike Forces was abolished. Attorney
General Robert Thornburgh felt there had been too many ‘turf
battles’ with US Attorneys into whose jealously guarded bailliwicks
the Strike Forces had intruded since 1967.18 Thornburgh argued
that the overall war on organized crime would be boosted if they
were absorbed within those same US Attorneys’ offices. However, as
the Strike Forces’ success could be measured in terms of the
hundreds of Mafiosi they had put in jail, it is hard to see how
‘disappearing’ them into offices which are susceptible to local
political pressures could enhance the war on mobsters, especially

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 364 ---
when it is these very same mobsters who so often create those
pressures. True, some of the greatest anti-Mafia cases have been put
together by US Attorneys, but these were in New York City under
exceptional Attorneys in extraordinary times. The norm throughout
America is far less impressive. Certain crime families have hardly
been touched. For decades no senior member of the Pittsburgh mob
has been put on trial. Merging the Pittsburgh Strike Force into the
US Attorney’s office has not yet improved that record.
But even if all the old Mafia families are in decline, and
vulnerable to takeovers from native Sicilians, does that make much
difference? After all, most of the old New York bosses – such as
Luciano, Bonanno, Profaci, Gambino and Castellano – were born in
Sicily. The only difference seems to be that the new breed are
smarter and even more ruthless than the old, which can hardly give
comfort to law-abiding Americans any more than it comforted John
Gotti to find out they had opened pizzerias in his own Brooklyn
neighbourhood.
They’re opening up all over the fuckin’ place, these Zips, ain’t
they? There’s three of them right on this avenue, here, they all
make money …They go around with hundreds of thousands in
their pockets, and you’re going around with your hat in your
hand.
As for the proposition that one Sicilian should be initiated into
Gotti’s family, the very idea gave the Dapper Don apoplexy:
Continental slime, that’s what he is. And European, ah, not
smarter than you’ll ever be … Ten years from now, a guy like a
Lorenzo, these young guys that we straightened out, they’re
gonna be really proud of these cocksuckers … And they are
gonna get stronger and stronger by our mistakes.
Far from weakening the old Mafia families, this evolution by
natural selection will only strengthen them. In Philadelphia this
process has seen the leadership of the family pass over a dozen
years from an old Sicilian, Angelo Bruno (shot because he had gone
‘soft’) to a new Sicilian, John Stanfa, who had been driving the car
in which Bruno was killed. Forty murders and six federal witnesses
on, the Philly family may be stronger rather than weaker – a

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 365 ---
thought which would give more comfort to Charles Darwin than to
the FBI.
For most Americans the ups and downs of particular crime
families are far less important than the nation’s fate at the hands of
organized crime as a whole. Honest citizens will take no comfort if
the Mafia is put out of business only to be replaced by even more
ruthless criminal brotherhoods.
In fact, this seems not to be happening. The brutal Colombian
drug cartels have shown no interest in laying down permanent roots
in the USA. Rather than develop a permanent immigrant network of
high-level fixers to match the Mafia’s, they are content using low-
level soldiers to smuggle their multi-billion dollar commodity on to
America’s streets. Why take risks themselves? Back home in
Colombia the Cali cartel bosses can buy the entire political system,
so they have no need to move to the USA. That fact should give
Americans no comfort, for these people have inflicted as much
drug-related misery from afar as if they had established an LCN-
style network on US soil. They have also bought up high-powered
American lawyers who seem only too willing to corrupt the legal
system for their poisonous clients. With that kind of start, it is
possible that in another thirty years Colombians might establish the
octopus-style corruptive power over America which the Mafia
achieved a couple of generations ago, but there is no sign of this
having developed yet.
In contrast, Chinese Tong and Triad societies have already spread
wherever there are Chinese communities. Some of these Mafia-style
groups mastermind huge imports of heroin. Others leech off straight
businessmen, but so far almost all their victims have been fellow
Chinese. By operating largely within their own communities, they
have avoided conflict with longer-established crime groups and kept
the interest of US law enforcement down to a minimum.
Since this book first appeared, one massive new crime grouping
has emerged worldwide. To claim organized crime has gone global
would be justified even if the only proof was the speed at which the
Russian Mafia (or Mafias) has spread throughout the old Soviet
empire and into western Europe. For nearly twenty years America
had a preview of this development, because of a 1974 law giving
asylum to Russian political refugees: this was brilliantly exploited
by racketeers who, far from fleeing oppression, came to America to
make fortunes oppressing others. With the collapse of Communism

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 366 ---
there was no longer any need for Russians to seek asylum, yet
Russian immigration did not cease. In fact it increased, so that by
the mid-1990s the total number of modern Russian immigrants had
reached 100,000. Most settled in Brighton Beach, Brooklyn where
the equivalent of a Mafia family had also set up its headquarters. By
1985, Marat Balagula had culled his way to the top of this family,
and was already making his fortune from a racket called the ‘gas tax
scam’. Balagula and Co. were not only selling bootleg petrol; they
were adding state and federal taxes totalling 28 cents per gallon
which (surprise, surprise) they did not hand to the tax collectors,
but pocketed themselves. By the time the scam was detected in
1986, the Russians were making hundreds of millions of dollars
every year.
It is curious that so obvious a scam had not been worked by the
Mafia long ago, but the Russians did have unique access to vast oil
supplies abroad. Even so, La Cosa Nostra took a cut of two cents a
gallon just for allowing the Russians to get on with it. The annual
do-nothing profit for four of New York’s five families was over $40
million. Meantime Russians have developed other crime specialities
across America: in counterfeit goods, bank and credit card fraud,
burglary and jewel theft. Sometimes Italian-American Mafiosi are
more than sleeping partners. The spectacle dazzles Pat Ryan, a
retired New York cop turned academic mob-watcher: ‘These days
many big jobs are pulled off by combinations of Italians and
Russians. Half the names end in vowels and half in “v”s!’
The potential mass immigration of tens of thousands more
Russian gangsters – with no visible criminal record, college-
educated and brighter than the average Mafioso – should cause
Americans serious concern. These hoodlums won’t knock out La
Cosa Nostra. They will become its partners, as they have already
become in eastern Europe. With the rise of new ‘Mafias’ throughout
the old Soviet empire, Sicilian gangsters have been buying vast
assets in states such as Hungary and Czechoslovakia, while also
shaking hands with the local crime bosses.
The late Claire Sterling (a visionary chronicler of organized
crime) believed these deals signalled ‘the birth of a worldwide
Mafia International’, in which the main shareholders are the Sicilian
and American Mafias and their east European counterparts.19 There
is also evidence that Chinese Triad and Japanese Yukusa gangs have
spent billions of dollars on legitimate investments in many US

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 367 ---
states, often with LCN connivance and co-operation. This trend will
probably continue, as the world’s crime fraternities have learned far
faster than the world’s police forces that co-operation is much
better than conflict.
There is now a United Nations of crime. Its massive assets have
permeated and corrupted banking, not just in miniscule Caribbean
island states but in Tokyo, London and New York. Money
laundering is now as big a business as organized crime itself, which
is why it mesmerises even great democratic nations and often
defeats their comprehension. There are many billions of ‘narco-
dollars’ alone slushing round the world’s financial systems, but most
is beyond seizure. You may trace hot money’s myriad movements
but how do you prove its crooked origins? Usually no one tries.
What bank will risk frightening off the deposit of even just a million
dollars by demanding to know how it was acquired? Still less a
billion. All kinds of disclosure and reporting laws are now imposed
on banks in many countries, but today so many dirty dollars are
circulating in world banking that they are part of its life-blood. If
they were all seized simultaneously, capitalism would have a
coronary. A similar health risk seems to have been spotted by
America’s hypochondriac politicians: the terminal shock which
occurs when law enforcement gets so smart in its analysis of
organized crime, and so skilful in pursuing it, that it proves the
crucial connection between gangsters and those same politicians.
For twenty-five years the Pennsylvania Crime Commission
performed outstanding service, investigating organized crime and
pursuing its perpetrators. Its reports were masterpieces in the art of
raising public awareness. They were also the most valuable source
books on organized crime ever released by a public body anywhere
in America.20 But when the Commission revealed it was
investigating claims that a leading politician had solicited funds
from organized crime, it was soon fighting for its life.
Back in 1987, ambitious Ernie Preate was District Attorney of
Lackawanna County, but he had a problem: how to pay off the
$80,000 he owed over his 1986 campaign. He turned to operators
of illegal video-poker gambling machines. In return for donations of
around $1,000 each, Preate promised not to enforce the law against
the contributors: their bars would not be raided and their machines
would not be seized. The donations were well spent, for
Lackawanna includes Scranton, one of Pennsylvania’s biggest cities,

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 368 ---
where there are rich pickings for gambling operators. Each poker
machine was earning $1,000 every week, and these guys had
dozens of them!
Preate asked them for contributions again in 1988 when he was
standing as the Republican candidate for Attorney General of all
Pennsylvania. Most machine owners payed up but two months later
the state police (who knew nothing of the deal) raided them
anyway. Preate was still just County DA so he had no authority over
these officers, but they had been obliged to give him advance
warning of the raids. His reaction was bizarre. According to one
trooper, ‘He was violently upset, throwing things around, cursing
and screaming. [He told me] I wasn’t getting the search warrants
and I wasn’t picking machines up … It wasn’t going to happen, not
in Lackawanna County.’21 Shortly after this encounter, the
operators were tipped off. ‘Machines were flying out of bars all over
town’ – but the State Police still seized 300 of the 400 machines
they had targeted.
Five days later, some of the raided operators received refunds
from ‘The Friends of Ernie Preate Committee’. None said anything
of their raid-free deal with friend Ernie, a silence which allowed
him to continue his political ascent. Six months later, he won
election as Attorney General, in which post he had overall authority
over the video-machine prosecution. When the case finally came up,
his office offered the operators a deal: they would plead guilty (as
corporations) to minor gambling offences instead of being
prosecuted (as individuals) for serious crimes under RICO, which is
what Preate’s own senior prosecutors had recommended. The
favour meant that, instead of going to jail for up to ten years, they
were merely fined. The deal staggered even one defence lawyer,
who could see there was plenty of evidence to convict them all on
‘corrupt organizations’ charges.
In 1992 Preate was re-elected Attorney General and was well
placed to win nomination for Governor, but by this time the Crime
Commission had proof of the illegal funds and crooked deals. The
dirt had surfaced because Preate’s main contributor was Elmo
Baldassari, the gang boss who was bigger than even the Mafia in
Scranton. Elmo was a longstanding buddy of Preate’s. He was also
capable of telling a debtor, ‘Ten fuckin’ minutes you got. I’m going
to kill you today. If that check ain’t here, you’re going to be
dead.’22 In a sense Preate was also his debtor, for the Baldassari

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 369 ---
clan and all the other operators under Elmo’s control had handed
Preate’s campaign $40,000. And yet Ernie had been unable to
deliver the raid-free deal he had promised. No wonder that when
Elmo ran into the DA at a fund-raiser in a Catholic church centre,
he bawled him out as ‘a fucking bum’.
By 1992 Elmo was even more upset because he was languishing
in jail for extortion while Ernie – the man he claimed to have made
– was now the top law man in Pennsylvania. As soon as Elmo
spilled the truth, all the other racketeers confessed they too had
helped fund the Attorney General.
When Preate found out about the Commission’s investigation, he
squealed that it was abusing its powers and pursuing a ‘political
witch-hunt’. He campaigned to shut it down and won the support of
many other state politicians. In December 1993 the legislature
voted to abolish the Commission, but in its final six months it
produced such strong proof of Preate’s crooked dealings that he was
defeated in his campaign for Governor. The case against him was
taken up as a Federal prosecution and in June 1995 Ernie pleaded
guilty to a catch-all offence of mail fraud, rather than face trial for
racketeering, conspiracy and obstruction. He was sentenced to
fourteen months and a fine of $25,000.
This was a bittersweet triumph for the Commission, for back in
June 1994 all its forty-five staff had lost their jobs. True, it had cost
$2.5 million a year to run, but in return it had saved the state
incalculable sums by curbing the activities of hundreds of mobsters
of all races. More to the point, perhaps, for twenty-five years it had
repeatedly exposed politicians: for their inertia over toxic waste
dumping; for their role in some cities where they were the
racketeers; for doing nothing about mob infiltration of legitimate
business; for tolerating police corruption which was so bad that
cops posed a greater crime problem than the gangsters they were
‘shaking down’. No wonder many Pennsylvanian politicians wanted
to abolish the Commission. As one citizen told a newspaper, maybe
they were ‘worried they would be the target of its next
investigation’.
The Penn CC’s shut down seems like a throw-back to the bad old
days of big city gangster-politicians like Hinky Dink, Bathhouse
John and the racketeers of Tammany Hall. It seems less incredible
that this could happen in the 1990s when you take into account
that the Commission was investigating the untouchable issue of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 370 ---
campaign financing – enough to give any American politician the
‘shakes’. The Commission had exposed how racketeers collude with
public officials to ‘enrich themselves at the expense of the citizenry’.
If the Commission had not existed, it would never have persisted
in getting Elmo Baldassari to reveal his crooked set-up with Preate.
If ‘Ernie the Attorney’ had not been busted, he would almost
certainly have become Governor, but he would not have been
satisfied with Pennsylvania. He was already on a key Washington
Drug Policy Board, and he would surely have considered running
for the White House. Had he become President, he would not have
been the first sponsored by gangsters. So, for want of the Penn CC,
another stooge of organized crime could have become leader of the
most powerful nation in the world.
The shut-down of the Penn CC is a symptom of a disease hitting
organized crime-fighting agencies all over America. It goes beyond
the abolition of the Strike Forces in 1990 to the closure or
downgrading of state agencies in New York, New Jersey, Florida,
New Mexico, Colorado and California, as funds have been
withdrawn or investigators have been ‘put on a short leash’. If this
reflects a political perception that America has beaten the mob, it is
mistaken. If it reflects politicians’ paranoia that all their campaign
contributions and secret pay-offs are going to be scrutinised with
the dedication of the Penn CC, then such closures are yet another
fraud on the voters.
If this withdrawal of funds reflects a vote-grabbing switch of
resources to ‘taking back the streets’ from dope-dealers and thieves,
again the politicians are deceiving the public. Reclaiming the streets
is a goal long overdue but it can be achieved only in tandem with
the continuing fight against organized crime. Declaring war on
street criminals, who are perceived to be mostly black, will achieve
little if pressure is taken off mobsters, who are mostly white. But, of
course, the black underclass has no political clout whereas LCN has
survived so long largely because of its carefully cultivated ability to
reach out to politicians, even in the White House.
Tom Paine, a great Pennsylvanian albeit an Englishman, once
said that to be successful in Britain’s corrupt system of elections, a
candidate ‘must be destitute of the qualities that constitute a just
legislator, and being thus disciplined by the mode of entering into
parliament, it is not to be expected that the representative should
be better than the man’.23 He might have been describing America’s

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 371 ---
present-day system of election funding, which almost invites
candidates to sell their souls to criminals looking for crooked
favours in return. And who is the worse threat to democracy? The
crook who doesn’t pretend to be anything else, or the crook who
pretends to be a paragon of virtue and runs for public office?
Well, so long as there are bent politicians, there will always be
crooks to take commercial advantage. For the past seventy years the
arch-corrupters in many American states have been the bosses of
LCN organized crime. So even though John Gotti may never stride
the streets of New York again in his designer suits, he could still
say, with certainty,
And this is gonna be a Cosa Nostra till I die. Be it an hour from
now or be it tonight or a hundred years from now when I’m in
jail. It’s gonna be a Cosa Nostra

29
Organized Crime Goes Global
At the start of the third millennium, governments and police forces
all over the world recognise that organized crime exists. They have
come at least that far in the half-century since J. Edgar Hoover’s
blanket denial that there was such a thing. The Mafia, Yakusa,
Colombian cartels and Russian ‘Mafias’ – all syndicates that know
no frontiers – are now the joint target of state-sponsored crime-
busting conferences in every continent. At such gatherings,
presidents and prime ministers utter fine words about putting these
firms out of business. Resolutions are passed, conventions signed
and a few headlines get printed. Then everyone goes home and
seems to forget the idea. Domestic priorities take over, elections
must be won and our high-minded crime-busting statesmen quickly
revert to type. When they are back on the stump hustling for votes
they mouth all the old clichés about cracking down on street crime,
but they do little to curb the trans-national gangs that ship the
drugs that end up on the very streets where the crime they are
campaigning against is committed – mostly by addicts trying to
fund their habits. It is a sad truth that politicians get better TV
coverage with rants about putting petty thugs behind bars than with
practical steps to combat faraway mobs with strange-sounding
names.
There is an alarming explanation for this. In many countries
today leading politicians are never going to fight organized crime
because they themselves are organized crime. The corruption of
national leaders now constitutes a bigger threat to nations than
traditional crime syndicates. This threat becomes overwhelming
when those leaders supplant the syndicates with their own crooked
government and party machines, utilising the power of their office
and the outward respectability of banks, big business and firms of
accountants to carry out fraud, drug-money-laundering and
sometimes theft of the entire state treasury.
How can law-abiding American citizens get seriously worked up

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 373 ---
about the antics of the Mafia (now reduced to a joke by TV series
such as The Sopranos and films like Analyse This) when they see
huge corporations collapsing with billion-dollar debts while top
officials divert millions of dollars into their own pockets? All this
within a framework of huge election fund donations to politicians
such as the man who would be president, George W. Bush.
And whilst the bosses of certain financial giants in the USA and
elsewhere are not guilty of murder – or physical violence or
intimidation – they appear to be getting away with it, figuratively
speaking. Recent years have seen the assassination of America’s
capitalist ideal, as financial corruption at the very highest level has
unleashed a new variant of the virus of organized crime, and the
slaughter of the financial dreams of millions of ordinary citizens –
employees, pensioners and small shareholders alike – who have put
their trust in the probity of such organizations.
At the turn of the century Enron’s seemingly unstoppable rise
from a small natural gas producer to number seven in the Fortune
500 made it one of US capitalism’s great success stories. In just a
few years its revenues had risen to $100 billion a year, putting it
way ahead of vast and venerable corporations such as AT&T; and
IBM. Exploiting opportunities in energy and derivatives markets
where, it seems, only the law of the jungle applied, Enron’s
controllers ran amok doing deals involving ‘off-balance-sheet
partnerships’ and ‘special-purpose entities’. They beguiled the gas
and electricity industries with techno-babble claims of using the
internet to match sellers with buyers keen to limit their exposure to
fluctuations in energy prices and supply Soon Enron was itself
setting prices in these markets, while simultaneously boosting its
own market value to a staggering $90 a share.
It was all hype and nonsense. Although Enron was involved in
almost a quarter of all America’s electricity and natural-gas
transactions, the company was making little real money. Its
accounts were seemingly specious, for it did not record all of its
vast debts and liabilities on its balance sheets. The fake profits were
invested in obscure utilities in distant lands and in new areas of
speculation such as ‘weather derivatives’. All this was connived at
and covered up by its esteemed firm of auditors, Arthur Andersen,
which gave it a clean bill of health. Only later did it emerge that
Enron was paying Andersen far bigger fees than was normal. In
return, Andersen turned a blind eye to Enron’s smoke-and-mirrors

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 374 ---
way of doing business, Meantime the banks that were earning vast
fees from underwriting Enron’s many merger deals – JP Morgan
Chase and Citigroup – had large sums of their own invested in
Enron, blunting their enthusiasm for questioning its practices. In
1999 a senior Citigroup loan executive, James F. Reilly, warned his
colleagues that the bank’s documentation could not honestly reflect
its dealings with Enron as this would ‘unfavourably alter the
accounting’.
In 2001 it all fell apart. Enron’s shares tumbled from $90 to just
36 cents, a fall of $75 billion in its market value. Meantime its
liabilities had risen to more than $40 billion. Yet only the year
before, Enron’s chairman, Kenneth Lay, had collected $132 million
in bonuses and options. He was also due a pension of $500,000 a
year even if Enron were to go bankrupt. In all, Enron executives ‘in
the know’ pocketed well over a billion dollars before the firm went
belly-up.
And yet it seems that Enron’s dealings were not illegal. However
reprehensible they were, they occurred in fields of financial activity
for which no rules had ever been written, where no existing law
applied. This was no comfort for the main victims of Enron’s
meltdown: tens of thousands of employees, pensioners and ‘mom
and pop’ shareholders, many reduced to penury by these
shenanigans. They may be forgiven for failing to figure out how
Enron’s major players broke no law and never went to jail, despite
perpetrating a massive fraud.
In a sharp summary of the story the Observer columnist Nick
Cohen wrote, Enron ‘appeared to be better protected than a Mafia
don. Bill Clinton begged Ken Lay, Enron’s chairman, for cash – then
ordered the American state to act as Enron’s agent and harry
governments in Britain, India and Africa […] After the company fell
apart, Americans discovered that 212 of the 248 members of the US
Congress investigating Enron had taken the corporation’s shilling.’
According to the Centre for Responsive Politics, a Washington-
based watchdog, Enron had spent a total of $5.8 million on federal
elections in the previous 12 years, almost three-quarters of it going
to Republicans. Seventy-one out of 100 US senators and 188 out of
435 House members benefited from its largesse. These donations
won Enron the influence it wanted. Twice during the Clinton
Presidency the Internal Revenue Service tried to get Congress to
close a loophole known as ‘trust-preferred securities’ which Enron

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 375 ---
was using to conceal its debts while avoiding tax on its phantom
profits. In a collective payback for Enron’s pay-offs Congress
rejected the move, thus blocking the IRS from trapping these scam-
merchants at a time when ordinary people would have lost far less
than they eventually did.
When George W. Bush took over at the White House, Enron’s
bosses must have felt they were home free. The new administration
was packed with former Enron officials, as well as major Enron
shareholders and beneficiaries of its generous funding. In the eight
years since he first ran for governor of Texas, Bush himself had
received
$826,000
from
Enron
which,
conveniently,
was
headquartered in his home state. He was on such good terms with
company boss Kenneth Lay that he called him ‘Kenny Boy’. In
return Enron secured a change in the law on derivatives to benefit
its doubtful trading methods. It also had six meetings with vice-
president Dick Cheney (another Enron beneficiary) whose policy
team was just drawing up a controversial energy plan to deregulate
US production and allow oil drilling in sensitive environmental sites
such as the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge. But when a conscience-
stricken whistle-blower within the company first exposed the truth
neither George ‘Dubya’ nor any of his appointees could save it from
collapse.
Though the antics of Enron were not strictly illegal, they showed
that robber barons could still loot the US economy and take from
people’s pockets with impunity. In recent years traditional
organized crime has been rather less profitable. Indeed, in
comparison La Cosa Nostra appears benign. At least it still provides
Americans with the illegal goods and services that they require, as
against Enron which just stole from them.
I have included this brief account of the Enron affair not to make
the facile observation that capitalism unrestrained is legalised theft
– as if this would legitimise organized crime – but to point out why,
to the millions of Americans who risk jail for petty breaches of the
drug laws, the system stinks. Some three million grow their own
marijuana. In 2001 724,000 were arrested for marijuana-related
offences. At any one time 50,000 are in jail because of marijuana,
out of a total prison population of a staggering two million. Not
only does this brutal oppression of drug-users sit ill alongside the
law’s blind eye towards schemers in outfits such as Enron. It looks
no better when considered alongside Washington’s involvement

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 376 ---
with regimes that traffic tons of hard drugs into America, that loot
the wealth of their own nations with impunity, and that use US
banks to launder the proceeds.
In the words of Dr. Ignacio Huertas, a Mexican economist who
lectures at New York University, ‘during the Clinton years,
international speculators literally looted Mexico’s richest assets,
buying up “privatized” public companies such as the national
airlines, the telecommunications system, even oil exploration rights,
at nominal prices set by the corrupt Salinas administration.’
Rather than stump up a fair price for stripping Mexico of its legal
assets, American financiers paid off the government. At the same
time some of country’s best connected villains needed a mechanism
through which to launder their vast criminal drug revenues. They
turned to Citibank – the same bank that later proved such a comfort
to Enron – where a specially briefed vice president transferred
briefcases of drug cash into sanitized Swiss bank accounts. By this
means Citibank laundered at least $140 million for one single
Mexican. He was no mere bandido. He was Raul Salinas, the
brother of Mexico’s elected president, Carlos Salinas. During
Carlos’s rule, Raul was Mexico’s number one drug trafficker, thanks
in part to the facilities extended to him by Citibank.
From 1992 to 1995 a senior Citibank officer, Amy Elliott, moved
a total of $100 million for Raul Salinas. In effect Elliott was Raul’s
personal
banker.
She
established
accounts
and
supervised
transactions for him at Citibank and other facilities in Mexico City,
New York, London, Zurich and Geneva.
The truth of all this started to come out only in March 1996
when Andres Oppenheimer of the Miami Herald reported that
federal grand juries in Washington D.C. and New York were
gathering evidence including records subpoenaed from Citibank.
Oppenheimer said that, as this is America’s second largest bank, this
emerging scandal had ramifications far beyond possible violations
of US money-laundering laws and the duty of all banks to report
any client activity looking remotely like washing dirty money.
Before Amy Elliott came under suspicion she had displayed
amazing nerve by testifying in the trial of a rival outfit, American
Express Bank International. She claimed that the money-laundering
which had allegedly occurred in the Amex outfit could never
happen in Citibank. She told the jury she had never managed funds
for ‘former’ Mexican public officials and that her bank’s policy of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 377 ---
knowing its private banking customers involved visiting them ten to
twelve times a year In their own countries: ‘This is why we go to
their homes, this is why we visit with their family, this is why we
go to their business, this is why we remember birthdays…. It’s too
risky not to do due diligence, not to know who you’re dealing
with…’.
Elliott, a Cuban-American, had begun her Citibank career in
1967 in its human resources department. Sixteen years later she
was appointed head of the ‘Mexico team’ for international private
banking. Her evidence in the Amex case gave just a hint of what
Citibank’s top echelon may have known about the transactions she
had OK’d for Raul Salinas. She said that when a new customer
‘prospect’ comes to the bank, ‘you will be sure to be asked, “Who is
this person, what do they do, who introduced them to you,” by at
least three or four people higher than you. It’s just the way it is.’
Consider the overall circumstances, as summarised by the
specialist publication, Money Laundering Alert, in April 1996. ‘The
brother of a country’s president is accepted as a customer in that

executive-level

of

Money Laundering Alert added that ‘no conclusions can presently
be drawn about the knowledge or involvement of high-ranking

command that linked Elliott to the folk at the very top.
‘Some Citibank officials whose duties put them in a position to
know about Salinas’ relationship with the bank transferred to other

that there was nothing for him to be suspicious about. After all,

However, in Mexico City it was well-known that Raul Salinas was

accounts through Citibank employee Amy Elliott, and second by
