accounts day by day.

Across the Atlantic in Africa, Citibank’s performance has also had

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 379 ---
its lapses. In June 1998 General Sani Abacha, the military ruler of
Nigeria, died of a heart attack. That’s the sanitised version. The
truth may lie closer to the story that he was given some Viagra pills,
went into action with three Indian prostitutes and died of over-
exertion whilst in flagrante delicto.
Whatever his sexual prowess, natural or artificially induced, the
General is now best known for looting billions of dollars from
Nigeria’s central bank, He was in power for just four-and-a-half
years but his personal ‘take’ has been estimated at $3 billion,
hoarded in banks abroad, most beyond the reach of any successor
government.
Not that Abacha had been entirely selfish. He had spread the loot
around. Several ministers and prominent businessmen shared in the
pillage, but his unexpected death did for them. When he expired
and his larceny was exposed, they would face charges of
embezzlement, fraud and money-laundering, and the many
properties they had bought with stolen cash would be seized.
Most of Abacha’s own loot would never be recovered. With as
much as $5 billion stolen from the treasury of this potentially rich
land, the consequences for its people were dire. General Ishola
Williams, a retired army commander was appalled: ‘As the biggest
oil producer in Africa we should have been rich but instead people
are living without clean water, electricity or phones because all our
money was stolen and sent overseas.’
The folk who benefited most from Abacha’s thievery were his
own family. This became clear in the most public way on the day
his widow, Maryam, turned up at Lagos airport with a pile of excess
luggage. No longer insulated from searches by officialdom, she was
subject to rigorous inspection. The outcome? The dead president’s
wife was found to have 38 cases stuffed with foreign currency to
the value of $300 million.
So where was she flying with all this loot? Where was she going
to put this massive sum? What was her destination? Saudi Arabia.
To date the Nigerian authorities have not found out exactly
where Maryam was planning to deposit this fortune but it is likely
that banks would have played a role in her scheme. There are many
banks in Saudi Arabia – Saudi and foreign – but few that could have
swallowed £300 million without getting indigestion.
It’s now been discovered that Abacha and his cronies used many
western banks to wash their loot. In 2001 Britain’s Financial

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 380 ---
Services Authority revealed that a three-month inquiry had found
evidence that £1 billion had been moved through 42 accounts set
up for Abacha and his family in 23 banks. Fifteen of the banks had
‘significant’ control weaknesses and the FSA had set seven of them
strict deadlines to rectify their procedures for combatting money-
laundering.
The entire probe showed that, even after spending 20 years
allegedly helping the war against drug-trafficking, many of the
world’s biggest banks were ill-equipped to combat criminal money
movements. Indeed, the bigger the transaction the more likely they
would accept it without informing local police agencies. For
example,
Britain’s
National
Criminal
Intelligence
Service
complained that some ‘highly respected’ banks make no disclosures.
Swiss banks were equally implicated in the Abacha clan’s
activities, with $213 million of Abacha ‘assets’ moved from London
to Switzerland, where a total of £430 million held in Abacha-
controlled accounts was frozen, with a view to its ultimate return to
Nigeria.
In September 2000 the veteran Guardian investigator, David
Pallister, produced a devastating article headlined, ‘PENNIES FROM
HEAVEN – MANY OF NIGERIA’S MISSING MILLIONS WERE LAUNDERED THROUGH
GREEDY BANKS IN LONDON’. This described how ‘two smart young
Nigerian businessmen’ appeared at Citibank’s private banking office
in London saying they were Ibrahim and Mohammed Sani, the sons
of Zachary Abacha, ‘a well-connected and respected member of the
northern Nigerian community’, according to the Citbank employee
who dealt with them, Michael Mathews. To him they were
‘delightful fellows’ and they rapidly became even more delightful as
‘tens of millions of dollars flowed through their coded accounts […]
by 1998 they had $60 million on deposit.’
Pallister described how the Sani brothers claimed their father
had given them authority to handle his money. They said they were
commodity and oil dealers (they traded substantial quantities of
sugar and steel) but did not reveal that they were also receiving
large bribes to facilitate government contracts. They went on to
open another account with Citibank in the USA. London’s Michael
Mathews had recommended them to a New York colleague, Alain
Ober, as ‘clearly target market by association, and the section of the
Nigerian community that we should be dealing with.’ The Sani
brothers said they needed this account to handle transactions

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 381 ---
related to their airline, then flying between Lagos and New York:
‘Within three years those accounts rose to contain $35m, even
though the airline had actually ceased operating.’
It seems that all this time Citibank was unaware, or did not
consider it relevant, that in 1988 their clients’ father was a general
in Nigeria’s army and chairman of the joint chiefs of staff. Two
years later he was defence minister and in 1993 he staged a coup
and became the country’s president. Now the money really flowed.
Pallister estimated that in all his clan looted $4.3 billion from the
state which the brothers and others ‘spread effortlessly around the
international banking system.’ Later Mohammed Abacha would
admit that he alone had received more than $700 million in cash
from his father.
The scale of this plunder drove the US Senate sub-committee on
investigations, chaired by Senator Carl Levin, to declare that
Citibank’s London and New York staff in the 1990s had been more
concerned with ‘customer deference than due diligence.’ The sub-
committee found account documents indicating that both Mr
Mathews and Mr Ober had performed due diligence reviews before
taking the Abacha sons as clients, but it was ‘unclear’ whether
either Citibank staffer recognised them as the sons of Nigeria’s head
of state until years after he had seized power.
Alain Ober testified that he found this out only in 1995 but,
according to a ‘public figure review list’ produced by Citibank in
London in May 1996, Mohammed Sani was still being described
merely as the ‘son of the minister for youth and sport’. It took
another year for an updated list to reveal he was the ‘son of
Nigerian president’. But, of course, such funds could generate large
profits which in turn would help boost the Citicorp share price and
make billions for the group’s biggest shareholders.
So what of those charming brothers? Ibrahim, the elder, died in
an air crash in 1995. And, as David Pallister reported back in 2000,
‘Mohammed now sits in Kiri Kiri maximum security prison in Lagos,
accused in connection with the murder of a wife of Moshood
Abiola, the winner of the annulled 1993 presidential election who
died in custody. Maybe in future banks in Britain will try to find out
more about charming and reliable clients from nasty dictatorships
who come with lots of dosh. Maybe.’

As for the Citibank story, it got curiouser and curiouser. In

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 382 ---
November 1999 its chairman John Reed told the US Senate
investigations sub-committee that Citibank had brought in new
procedures to combat the revelations that Raul Salinas, Sani Abacha
and indeed former Pakistani premier, Benazir Bhutto, had all kept
accounts in the bank. Reed claimed that it had now adopted a
written policy establishing ‘special criteria for approval of accounts
for public figures.’
But in 2000 a new scandal hit Citibank: staggering claims of
corruption about Peru’s triple-term president Alberto Fujimori. The
most compelling evidence were video tapes secretly recorded by his
confidante, strongman and spymaster, Vladimiro Montesinos. These
showed Montesinos himself, a balding lawyer in his fifties, making
140 corrupt payments on Fujimori’s behalf to some of the country’s
highest ranking officials. When one tape was stolen and shown on
television, the President fled to Tokyo, only deigning to inform the
Peruvian people of his resignation by fax. As for Montesinos, he
sailed first to the Galapagos Islands – confirming the theory of an
earlier visitor, Charles Darwin, that only the fittest survive – thence
to Venezuela. Repatriated in handcuffs in June 2001, he went on
trial in 2003 facing 57 charges for money-laundering, bribery, drug-
trafficking, deathsquad killings and sending arms to guerrillas in
neighbouring Colombia. Today a further 1,300 well-placed
Peruvians are under investigation for corruption in the same affair.
They include a dozen generals, several ministers, some newspaper
and TV station owners and two of Mr Montesinos’ lovers.
Montesinos was not a good egg suddenly gone bad. As far back
as the 1970s he had been thrown out of the army with ignominy,
spent a year in jail and was lucky not have gone on trial for treason
for selling military secrets to the USA. Despite this, under Fujimori
he was put in charge of all Peru’s armed forces. At the same time he
had managed to smuggle at least US$207 million into foreign bank
accounts. According to the FBI, he had stolen over $100 million
from the nation’s military pension fund. But though he has been
brought to justice, ex-President Fujimori is still in Japan. At the
time of writing, Peru has yet to demand his extradition, partly
because the Supreme Court has still not translated the relevant files
into Japanese.
The FBI had been taking a particular interest in Montesinos,
partly because of the energetic activities of his ‘most trusted bag/
straw man’, Victor Venero Garrido. On behalf of both Montesinos

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 383 ---
and Fujimori, Venero had been laundering their ill-gotten gains
through accounts in – yes, you’ve guessed it – Citibank. When the
FBI arrested Venero outside a Citibank branch in Miami, it
discovered that he had recently deposited $15 million in that
branch alone.

Peru, Mexico, Nigeria are just a few of the states where leading
American and British banks have recently been caught up in the
money-laundering activities of dictators and their drug-running
relatives. But banks are only one element in the continuing advance
of organized crime. An even greater contributor to the cause of
international gangsterdom is the government of the United States of
America, especially through its military intervention in the world’s
two
most
notorious
drug-producing
states:
Colombia
and
Afghanistan.
America’s commitment to helping the Colombian government in
its war against the Marxist ‘Revolutionary Armed Forces of
Colombia’ – FARC – has the smack of virtue, especially as both
governments claim FARC is in league with the country’s cocaine
traffickers and derive much of its funding from the trade. Even if
this is true, it shows that, as with so much else in US foreign policy,
the road to hell is paved with good intentions.
In recent years America has allocated well over one billion
dollars to Colombia’s war on drugs and terror. It committed a
further $100 million when President Bush signed the ‘Iraq aid bill’
in April 2003. At the same time President Uribe was in talks with
the right-wing paramilitary force that calls itself the AUC: the
‘United Self-Defense Forces of Colombia’. Nicknamed ‘the Head
Splitters’ and ‘the Chain Saws’, in tribute to how it kills its victims,
the AUC is just as murderous as the FARC, which controls a third of
the country largely through terror. In the 1990s the AUC’s leaders,
the Castano brothers, killed two presidential candidates. The US
State Department knows the AUC is just as bad as the FARC. Only
one day before the 9/11 attack it designated the AUC a ‘Foreign
Terrorist Organization’ because of its ‘numerous acts of terrorism,
including the massacre of hundreds of civilians, the forced
displacement of entire villages and the kidnapping of political
figures to force recognition of AUC demands.’ In all, Colombia’s
civil war and the lawlessness around it is causing 26,000 violent
deaths a year.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 384 ---
So we now have a situation where America is backing Colombia’s
president more strongly than ever in his war on left-wing terror just
as the president’s representatives are discussing a deal with a right-
wing terror group that would grant it immunity from prosecution
for its horrific past crimes. This deal is as pragmatic as it is cynical.
In the words of Robin Kirk, a painstaking chronicler of human
rights abuses on all sides in Colombia, the AUC ‘could never have
grown as much or as swiftly as they did – or caused as much grief –
without the military’s support’. Explaining what drove her to write
her 2003 book, More Terrible Than Death, she says that much of the
profit from the estimated $46 billion a year that America’s six
million users spend on cocaine and heroin ‘is plowed directly back
into Colombia’s war […] our addictions pay for terror and grief.
Our parties pull Colombia under, as surely as a stone sinks cloth in
water. Most of all, our failure to acknowledge our behaviour and
examine its real consequences means that there is little hope that
Colombia will ever be able to pull itself out of the violence that for
decades has marked the lives of Its citizens. At a time when
Americans are more aware of the impact our actions have in the
world, we are still largely deaf and blind to our havoc in Colombia.’
Nor will America’s combined war on terrorism and drugs succeed
in suppressing both activities for these goals are conflicting and
contradictory. The Medellin and Cali cartels that dominated the
cocaine trade until the early 1990s have been largely suppressed
but the trade continues. Today it is run by many small groups
which together are exporting as much cocaine into the USA as the
cartels they have replaced. They smuggle over 600 tons a year out
of Colombia. The FARC may take hundreds of millions of dollars as
its cut, for it controls much of the area in which the coca leaf is
produced, but there is nothing intrinsically Marxist about drug-
trafficking.
Indeed,
if
drug
traffickers
have
any
political
inclinations, they are more likely to be capitalist than socialist. As it
happens, the AUC is in part the child of Medellin’s drug barons. Its
founder, Fidel Castaño had belonged to the cartel. Though he is
now dead his brother Carlos, a convicted murderer, still picks up
hundreds of millions of dollars a year from the cocaine trade – as
much as the FARC.
As long as America allies itself to a Colombian government that
snuggles up to the AUC, it will not solve its own appalling drug
problem, for whenever the Colombian military’s Rapid Deployment

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 385 ---
Force drives the FARC from a drug-producing region, the AUC
merely takes over the trade. As Carlos Castaño explains, “I prefer
taking cash from the narcos than from honest people.” In 2002 he
was indicted in the USA on drug-trafficking charges but now he will
be America’s ally. What’s more, as Human Rights Watch told the US
Senate Foreign Relations Committee in 2002, now that it controls
40 percent of Colombian cocaine production, the AUC itself ‘funds
acts of terror. There is a direct connection, therefore, between the
profits from trafficking and human rights abuses.’

Much the same confusion surrounds America’s war on terror on the
other side of the world. Following 9/11 it was understandable and
inevitable that America would launch a war on the Taliban in
Afghanistan and its Al-Qaeda guests, notably Osama Bin Laden. The
result was a devastating air onslaught and the demolition of the
regime, although Bin Laden evaded capture. Overall, mission
accomplished, claimed Washington, but the intervention had
catastrophic consequences for its ‘war’ on organized crime.
While America may have detested the Taliban’s fundamentalist
interpretation of Islam, and certainly could not tolerate its support
for terror, the Taliban was doing one thing America should have
supported: its opposition to the opium trade had reduced
production to a mere 185 tons a year. But within a year of the
Taliban’s fall, in October 2002 the UN Office for Drug Control and
Crime Prevention reported that opium production in Afghanistan
had risen by 1800 percent to 3,400 tons. The crop was being grown
in 24 of the country’s 32 provinces, most of it in Badakhshan in the
north, Nangarhar in the east, Uruzgan in the centre, and Helmand
and Kandahar in the south – in other words, throughout the land
that America had ‘liberated.’
This boom had occurred despite a scheme costing £40 million
funded mostly by Britain, where some 80 percent of heroin for sale
has been manufactured from Afghan opium. The scheme failed
partly because much of the money never reached the farmers who
were meant to get it, to subsidise them planting far less profitable
crops. The new government, headed by America’s nominee, Hamid
Karzai, had continued the formal ban on poppy cultivation and
tractors were sent to destroy the crop, but only a few farmers got
the promised compensation because the first applicants had hugely
inflated their losses and scooped up the entire fund before the rest

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 386 ---
could be paid. The result? Where once Britain had been predicting
opium eradication within three years, it was now saying ten years.
Even that looks ludicrously optimistic as the Afghan government’s
writ barely extends outside Kabul, and the warlords beyond have no
intention of surrendering this huge source of income.
The warlords also know that the invaders will not confront them
over opium because of their overriding commitment to ensuring the
Taliban do not reassert themselves lest Al-Qaeda also re-surfaces.
Neither America nor Britain wants to alienate either the Northern
Alliance, which helped them overthrow the Taliban, or the Pashtun
tribes in the south and east. So going easy on Afghanistan’s opium
trade is the price that western nations must pay, unless they are
prepared to stump up the billions of dollars that are needed for the
country’s reconstruction, which looks most unlikely. Instead, Britain
will pay more than its share in the vast amounts that addicts spend
on Afghan-derived heroin in all our towns and cities, in medical
care for those addicts, in the cost to the lives, limbs and property of
the victims of crimes which addicts commit to fund their habit, in
the expense of putting these offenders on trial and in keeping them
in jail for year after year. Going on past form, we have no reason to
believe that the West has the stomach for a long campaign against
the opium/heroin trade any more than against cocaine. For the
foreseeable future, neither America nor Britain has the will or the
money to fight the war on terror and the war on organized crime
simultaneously. We have fought to liberate the people of
Afghanistan from one form of slavery, only to boost the number of
our own citizens enslaved to heroin, due to a production boom
sponsored by the same warlords that were our allies in war.
Not that the drug-producing states are the only miscreants. For
Afghan opium to reach western Europe it must pass through many
other countries in which governmental, judicial and police
corruption is rife. On the night of 3 November 1996 outside the
town of Susurluk in western Turkey a 600 SEL Mercedes travelling
at 110 miles per hour crashed into a truck, There were four people
in the car. Three died: the driver who was the celebrated police
chief of Istanbul, a gangster who belonged to the ‘Turkish mafia’,
and a former beauty queen who worked in a casino. The survivor
was a government MP called Sedat Bucak. As soon as the news
broke, every politically-conscious Turk was guessing what had
brought this motley crew together. And they were right: what else

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 387 ---
could unite a politician, a cop and a crook but money!
The ‘Susurluk Affair’ became a national scandal, with drug-
trafficking, murder and the security services all bound up together,
as it emerged that the true name of the dead gangster was Abdullah
Catli, a right-wing hitman facing a murder charge. All the more
curious, therefore, that on his dead body he was found to have a
diplomatic passport and an identity card in another name signed by
Turkey’s Minister of Interior when he had been a police chief.
Following this revelation, the minister was forced to resign, with
the obvious implication that Catli had been hired by the state in
special operations at home and abroad. This impression was
reinforced when Tansu Ciller, the deputy Prime Minister and leader
of the ‘True Path’ party, stood up in parliament and defended Catli
saying, ‘He is a hero who has fired shots and been shot at in defence
of this country.’
When it turned out that almost everyone involved in the affair
was connected to Ciller’s ‘True Path’ party, she resigned and the
government collapsed, Her husband faced charges over claims that
his yacht had been used for drug-smuggling and kidnapping, and
that members of what was now being called the ‘Susurluk gang’ –
including a senior figure in the security services – had gone into
hiding at a farm owned by the couple. Those charges were dropped
because of insufficient evidence, though they had been endorsed by
an MP who had sat on a parliamentary commission of inquiry. One
reason why there was insufficient evidence was that the MP who
survived the crash refused to testify
Only in February 2001 did a court convict anyone connected to
the Susurluk gang. when the former head of police special forces
and a senior intelligence agent were convicted of running a criminal
gang with the aim of ‘creating panic in society’. They received six-
year jail terms. Eleven other defendants got four years. The
prosecution revealed a shocking story of leading state officials
sanctioning political murders. The victims included left-wing
journalists
and
prominent
Kurds
dedicated
to
achieving
independence or, at least, real autonomy for this large minority that
forms the majority in south-east Turkey: the very region through
which much of the opium grown in Afghanistan and Pakistan is
smuggled. It was in return for the firee passage for drugs and a
blind eye to extortion that the ‘Turkish mafia’ conspired with the
nation’s secret service to kill anyone who stood in the way of their

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 388 ---
political allies. Catli’s targets included Armenian groups in France,
Greece and Canada that were waging a worldwide war against
Turkish targets. It emerged that for over 20 years Catli and fellow-
criminals belonging to an ultra-nationalist movement had been
performing the role of an unofficial SAS-style state assassination
squad. They even had links to the notorious murder of a journalist
in 1979, the attempt to kill Pope John Paul II in Rome in 1981, and
another plot 14 years later to kill the president of Azerbaijan.
The regime that ran and paid these assassins has fallen but the
masterminds behind so vast a conspiracy were never brought to
justice. Not that the long-suffering Turkish people ever thought they
would be. If opinion polls are anything to go by, they never
believed that the full truth would ever come out. On the other
hand, that regime was easily understood and embraced by the USA,
whose foreign policy traditions include turning a blind eye to the
excesses of a police state with a strong military, provided it stands
firm against America’s perceived enemies and especially if, like
Turkey it wears the mask of outward democracy.
Today Turkey has a largely Islamist government that America is
finding difficult to accept. But that it is the trouble with
democracies: occasionally they elect governments that reflect the
will of the people as a whole, rather than ones that automatically
serve US interests. In this case one surprise consequence was the
parliament’s refusal to permit US troops to cross Turkey to invade
northern Iraq. This provoked America to cut billions from an aid
package which Turkey sorely needed. But if America had not
backed previous governments that had formed a deadly alliance
with the Turkish Mafia, the Islamists might not be as strong as they
are today. But at least this government is less likely than its
predecessors to tolerate the mass importation of Afghan opium, its
refinement into heroin on Turkish soil and its export to Europe and
America. For that America should be grateful.

The interplay between right-wing politics and organized crime in
Turkey echoes what we have seen going on in Colombia, Peru and,
historically, in the USA. Arguably the country where this
phenomenon is most in evidence today is Russia where bribery,
corruption, blackmail and kidnapping are the stock-in-trade of
hundreds of local ‘mafias’. As Russian police officers get paid a
meagre $135 a month – less than £100 – entire forces can be

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 389 ---
bought wholesale. According to the think-tank, ‘Information for
Democracy’, Russian businesses pay more than US$30 billion a year
in bribes: equal to what the government hoped to collect in taxes
from the 2002 federal budget. Some 12 percent of the nation’s gross
domestic product is spent on bribes to state officials and pay-offs to
gangsters with a well-deserved reputation for kidnapping and
murder.
There are now over 12,000 crime gangs in Russia, so no wonder
they often get in each other’s way and indulge in spectacular shoot-
outs. These get headlines in the West, as do stories about gangs
killing Investigative reporters for exposing their rackets. But it is
their low-key day-to-day activities that bring them billions of
dollars a year at home and which they have transplanted so
successfully to America. Since I first referred to Russian ‘OC’ in my
postscript to the 1997 edition of this book, these immigrant gangs
have matched the dynamic growth of the Sicilian Mafia when it first
came to America. According to Walter Zalisko, a former New Jersey
police official turned specialist crime consultant, at least 30 Russian
gangs are now operating in America, Their activities include
‘burglary, credit card forgery, theft, auto theft, insurance and
medical fraud, fuel tax fraud, arson, prostitution and narcotics
trafficking. [They] conduct the most sophisticated criminal
operations ever seen in the United States, based on their access to
expertise in computer technology, encryption techniques and
money-laundering facilities that process hundreds of millions of
dollars.’
Zalisko goes on to say that they ‘tend to be highly educated, and
therefore, are very resourceful and sophisticated in their methods of
operation.’ Proof of this emerged in 2001 when two Russian
employees of the Bank of New York were suspended for allegedly
helping to launder billions of dollars through the bank on behalf of
leading Russian mobsters.
One of Russia’s most lucrative rackets is ‘sex slavery’, a trade
made easy by the sad fact that there is such high unemployment
among young women in Russia, the Ukraine and Belarus who they
are easily tempted abroad by adverts offering huge wages as
secretaries, maids, nannies or waitresses but, as soon as they are
smuggled abroad, they find themselves without valid travel papers
and in deep debt to the agents who recruited them. When these
men tell them their only way out is to work as prostitutes, any

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 390 ---
objections are rapidly overcome with the threat of violence. If they
go to the authorities, even in a democracy, the best they can hope
for is deportation back to their home country where they face
retribution from the pimps they had hoped to shake off.
Every year thousands of women are exported from eastern
Europe and the former Soviet Union to work as ‘white slaves’ in
Greece, Turkey, Germany, the Netherlands, Britain, the USA and
Israel. In 1998 Reuters correspondent Elisabeth Eaves wrote a
distressing account entitled, ‘Israel Not the Promised Land for
Russian Sex Slaves’, in which she cited estimates from the Israel
Women’s Network that 70 percent of prostitutes in Tel Aviv come
from the former Soviet republics, and about 1,000 women are
brought into Israel illegally each year. She told the story of one 17
year old from a Russian coal-mining city who had been told she
would get a job abroad. Soon after reaching Israel she was sold to a
pimp for $9,000, she was forced to have sex with an average of six
men a day seven days a week. They each paid $75 for an hour’s
pleasure but she got nothing except tips. In 1998 one Tel Aviv
brothel-owner told the New York Times, ‘Israelis love Russian girls.
They are blonde and good looking, and they are desperate. They are
ready to do anything for money.’
Easily smuggled in among thousands of Russian immigrants, such
girls have no way of getting out unless they escape and get to a
police station, from where they will be rapidly despatched to a
women’s prison to await deportation. This swift retribution is in
stark contrast to the lack of action taken against their pimps, even
though they could all be put on trial for rape, abduction, battery
and theft. What’s true of Israel is true of the many other countries
to which these women are transported. Worldwide, this business is
estimated to generate six billion dollars a year for Russian
organized crime. And it’s not just Russians in this trade. In London
it has taken less than five years for Albanian pimps to oust other
ethnic groups from the live sex trade in Soho and other vice
quarters. What gives them their edge is a readiness to use violence
and access to a seemingly endless supply of white women who cost
them almost nothing and have no way out.

Sex slavery is only part of the far wider racket of people-smuggling.
Viewed heartlessly as a commodity, sex-slaves can be ‘sold’
repeatedly and make a huge profit for their masters for several

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 391 ---
years, but the relatively new trade of smuggling asylum-seekers and
economic migrants across continents has the advantage of trading
in far greater numbers. It thrives on a mass turnover, for there is an
endless supply of desperate people willing to pay vast sums for just
one trip. A business that smuggles hundreds of thousands of people
from poor countries into rich ones requires organization and is, by
definition, run mostly by organized crime syndicates. But as the
demand is so great, widespread and diverse, it is a racket that no
one group or nationality can control. It is therefore both organized
and anarchic which makes it almost impossible to combat or defeat.
Whether the people are Mexicans and Haitians trying to get into the
USA (where there are now eight million illegal immigrants),
Afghans and Kurds trying to get into Britain, or Eritreans and
Somalis trying to make landfall in Italy, there is always room for
new entrepreneurs to make fortunes out of other people’s
desperation.
In June 2000, 58 young Chinese men and women were found
dead at Dover, England in a container that appeared to be carrying
tomatoes. Its Dutch driver had transported them on the relatively
short journey from Rotterdam but his illegal cargo had suffocated
because he had closed the air vent so they would not be heard by
the crew on the ferry from Zeebrugge. Only two people survived.
The middlemen, who would later be jailed, were Turkish and Dutch
but behind them was a ‘snakehead’ gang from mainland China.
Each migrant had paid £20,000, probably for a flight to Moscow,
then a land journey across Europe via Belgrade, only to meet a
terrible death. Ten months after this dreadful event – perhaps in
reaction to it – a government tribunal ruled that as many 10,000
illegal Chinese immigrants already in Britain would not be thrown
out because they could be killed by the Snakeheads if they were
sent back to China. This is the global face of organized crime today.
In comparison, La Cosa Nostra – the Mafia in America – looks
both gentlemanly and way behind the times. It can still spark into
life, but the FBI has its number, and now that John Gotti is dead –
expiring of throat cancer in jail aged just 61 – the old gang doesn’t
have quite the swagger, style, romance and brutality that the media
and especially Hollywood has come to respect and expect. But back
in Italy there are signs that the bitch that bore La Cosa Nostra is not
quite done for.
This truth spilled out from an unexpected quarter in August 2001

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 392 ---
when a member of the government of Silvio Berlusconi uttered a
staggeringly indiscreet remark. Pietro Lunardi was not only the
Minister for Infrastructure, he also owned a big engineering firm.
He was therefore a man well-versed in Italian business but a babe-
in-arms when it came to the fine political art of saying nothing that
can get you into trouble. What else can account for him saying how
organized crime, la malavita, would not stand in the way of Italy’s
economic development: ‘Everyone should resolve the problems of
criminality as they see fit […] The Mafia and the Camorra have
always existed and they always will. We have got to learn to live
with this reality.’
For a few days Italy was in uproar. Being told to live with the
Mafia and Camorra was no comfort to those they had killed, or to
their victims’ relatives, as the widow of a straight Sicilian
businessman made clear in a letter to Italy’s president: ‘The Mafia
kills, it deals in drugs, weapons and prostitution. Can a minister of
the republic acquiesce in all this?’
There was also concern that, if Lunardi stayed in the job long
enough, he could be taking key decisions over huge public work
projects, notably whether the bridge joining Sicily to the mainland
should at last be built. As organized crime tries to extort a chunk of
every state contract in southern Italy, and usually succeeds, Lunardi
seemed to be saying this cosy relationship between the underworld
and the overworld should continue.
If that was what he meant, other political figures were accused of
taking the relationship a stage further. At that moment, Prime
Minister Berlusconi’s closest ally in Sicily, Marcello Dell’Utri, was
on trial of charges of collaborating with the Mafia. Far more
notoriously, Giulio Andreotti, seven times prime minister and the
most durable character in Italian public life since World War Two,
was facing similar charges. He was finally cleared in 2003 but by
then he had been convicted of involvement in the murder of a
political journalist back in 1979. At 83 he is unlikely to serve any of
his 24-year jail sentence which drew Berlusconi to comment that
‘Andreotti is the victim of justice gone mad.’
In April 2003, Berlusconi’s former defence minister, Cesare
Previti, was jailed for 11 years for bribing judges to rig trials on
behalf of Berlusconi’s huge Fininvest media combine as it fought for
control of Italy’s biggest publisher, Mondadori. A few days later
Berlusconi was himself on trial, alongside Previti in Milan, on

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 393 ---
charges of giving judges £85,000 in the 1980s when they were
considering the privatisation of a state-owned food company that he
was trying to buy. In court he claimed that he and Previti were
victims of ‘judicial persecution’ by ‘red’ left-wing magistrates. He
then lashed out with a devastating claim that he had never had any
direct interest in the matter but that the prime minister of the day
Bettino Craxi, had begged him to step in to block a scheme to sell
the company at a ‘below-market’ price to another entrepreneur.
Berlusconi then impugned Romano Prodi, currently President of the
European Commission, who in 1985 headed the state-holding firm
in charge of the sale. Berlusconi stated that Prodi had knowingly
undervalued the value of the company at an ‘outrageous’ cut price,
implying that Prodi was the corrupt one. Prodi, himself a former
Italian prime minister, promptly denied the claim. ‘This is not my
trial,’ he said, while his supporters added, Berlusconi was ‘firing an
unloaded gun.’
Heady stuff, not likely to boost confidence in the governance of
Italy or the European Commission which Berlusconi would be
presiding over for the six months from July 2003, during which the
verdict in this case was to be announced. No wonder he fought to
re-introduce immunity from prosecution for MPs (succeeding just
one month earlier). Of course, he could have saved a lot of blushes
by standing down as premier while standing trial for corruption,
just as he could have detached himself from his business empire
while premier, but he did not become a billionaire and the richest
person in Italy by complying with convention.
All this sits ill alongside the brainchild that Berlusconi launched
at the controversial Group of Eight summit in Genoa in 2001: his
‘Fight Against International Crime’ programme. Bystanders might
think this would also mean a fight against corruption but it seems
he believes it means suppressing magistrates trying to investigate
corruption. He has been here before. In 1994 he had to step down
as Prime Minister when he faced trial for tax fraud. In 1998 he was
sentenced to 28 months in jail for passing £7.5 million to his ally,
Bettino Craxi – himself by then a fugitive from justice – through an
offshore account. Berlusconi has appealed, so he has still not gone
to jail, because in Italy the appeal process can take ten years. The
story continues. In 2001 a judge in Spain wanted to try Berlusconi
for another tax fraud, but as prime minister of Italy, he cannot be
extradited.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 394 ---
Perhaps the biggest shame is that George W. Bush and Tony Blair
were happy to embrace this man as their ally in the 2003 war
against Iraq, even though the vast majority of Italians were against
the war, but in the world of realpolitik, this should come as no
surprise.
Where does this leave the Mafia? Pietro Lunardi, the Minister for
Infrastructure, was only telling the truth when he blurted out that,
‘The Mafia and the Camorra have always existed and they always
will. We have got to learn to live with this reality.’ Neither
organization is quite what it was but both seem eternally capable of
regenerating themselves according to changing circumstances.
Today, as the menfolk of these ‘honoured societies’ have been
getting betrayed by pentiti and locked up in substantial numbers –
as well as slaughtered in the usual feuds – some of their women
have asserted themselves and taken over the business.
In Naples, birthplace of Vito Genovese and Al Capone’s mamma
and papa, gangland isn’t what it used to be. As Rory Carroll, the
Guardian’s correspondent has written, ‘the more mafiosos who are
put away, the more their women fill the vacuum. The godmothers,
police are realising, are just as ferocious as their men.’ In a report
headlined ‘Italy’s Most Wanted Mamma’ he catalogued the ferocious
achievements of Maria Licciardi, a local girl made bad when she
succeeded her father and brother as the crime boss of
Secondigliano, a Naples suburb. Blessed with ‘exceptional
intelligence, charisma, supernatural calm, the brains of a ruthless
tactician’, this middle-aged ‘diminutive godmother of crime’
schemed her way to the leadership of all 20 Camorra clans in the
city.
When gang deaths soared, she sought to cut the carnage by
promoting co-operation rather than competition but the plan
collapsed in 1999 when she banned the distribution of a shipment
of heroin from Turkey on the grounds that it was so pure it was
lethal. A rival clan ignored the warning and put it on the streets
anyhow. Soon junkies were dying on the streets. Straight citizens
were outraged. They had tried to clean up Naples’ violent image but
now it was back as bad as ever. Soon the dead weren’t just junkies.
A Camorra war had begun. When four members of her own clan
were shot, Maria ordered a string of revenge killings. Within
months the mutual slaughter had claimed 50 corpses and Maria had
become Italy’s most wanted mobster. She evaded capture, becoming

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 395 ---
a national heroine of sorts, dubbed ‘the Princess’ by Italy’s press,
whereas the dummer sex in her profession were becoming a joke.
When the police raided a dirty money investment summit in a
farmhouse outside the city, they apprehended 13 Camorra capi but
their main target, Maria, wasn’t there. According to Rory Carroll,
state officials, lawyers and bankers were all mixed up in the
investment scheme, confirming that in this land what’s good
enough for Berlusconi is good enough for the mob.
Maria Licciardi may be the most notorious and celebrated
modern mafiosa but there are others in Naples, Calabria and across
Sicily. Their emergence is not proof of some mutation of the sexes
in the Mezzogiorno. It’s just that most of the bosses of the Mafia,
Camorra and ’Ndrangheta (the name for Calabria’s Mafia) are now
locked up in jail. But if ever Italy’s straight politicians, magistrates
and police chiefs relax their grip or lose the will to fight la malavita
these gangsters will reassert themselves. At which point the women
will probably withdraw to running the business side of the
enterprise, leaving the men to slaughter each other in the usual way
But will they? In May 2002 a long-running feud between two
clans in the mountains above Naples exploded into a shootout that
left three people dead and two injured in one can. Nothing
remarkable, you might say – an everyday story of Camorra folk –
except that all six were women, including three as young as 16, 19
and 22. They had been ambushed by two other cars. One drove off
but the other was found to contain two more women, aged 19 and
22, also with gunshot wounds. According to the police, the feud
was over which clan would run the local extortion rackets and levy
the usual cut of public contracts. Murder and prison had removed
the men from the scene so the women had taken over. In the words
of the Corriere della Sera, the incident showed that ‘not only bosses
and their “soldiers” have the duty to eliminate members of rival
families; now the women, the bosses’ wives and even the daughters
participate.’
Oh! For the good old days of organized crime, when the men of
honour did the killing and their wives stayed indoors, barefoot and
pregnant.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 396 ---

1. Loftus’s role as the first policeman on the scene and the man who

Department, through Ron Koziol of the Chicago Tribune. This
elementary inquiry, which no one else appears to have made
since the Massacre, revealed that the Loftus role has been
wrongly attributed to a Detective Clarence Sweeney for the last
